British police arrest five men for allegedly preparing a terrorism attack with explosives on a base used by U.S. bombers for Iran war
2026-09-27

British police arrest five men for allegedly preparing a terrorism attack with explosives on a base used by U.S. bombers for Iran war

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
British police arrested five men on Sunday on suspicion of preparing an act of terrorism and explosives offenses outside RAF Fairford, a military base in western England that’s operated by the U.S. Air Force. Three suspicious vehicles are being searched, and counterterror officers are leading the investigation. RAF Fairford, about 100 miles (160 kilometers) west of London, is one of several bases in Britain that has been used by the U.S. Air Force under an agreement dating from the 1950s. It is home to the headquarters of the U.S. Air Force’s 501st Combat Support Wing and is a forward operating location for U.S. Air Force Global Strike Command. Fairford’s long runway makes it suitable for heavy bombers, and it has been used in conflicts including the Gulf War, the 2003 Iraq war and the U.S.-Israeli war on Iran this year. Britain did not join the U.S.-Israeli war on Iran that began Feb. 28 and initially denied President Donald Trump’s request to use bases in the U.K. to strike Iran. But then-Prime Minister Keir Starmer announced days later that he had agreed to let the U.S. use bases in Britain for “defensive” operations against Iran’s missiles and their launch sites following Iranian attacks on U.K. interests and Britain’s allies in the Gulf. American B-1 and B-52 bombers have taken off from Fairford for operations during the Iran conflict. Anti-war campaigners have protested outside the perimeter fence of the base several times since the start of the Iran war. It’s unclear whether the arrests are linked to the Iran war. British security officials say proxies linked to Iran are behind a string of arson and sabotage attempts in the U.K. and other countries, many of them targeting the Jewish community. They have previously warned that Tehran could expand its targets in the U.K. if conflicts in the Middle East continue. Some local residents said security appeared to have been heightened around RAF Fairford in recent days, with heavy vehicles parked across entrance gates. Another American base, RAF Lakenheath in eastern England, confirmed that it is on Charlie alert, the second-highest threat level that can be issued by the U.S. Department of Defense. According to U.S. military websites, Force Protection Condition Charlie “applies when an incident occurs or intelligence is received indicating some form of terrorist action targeting personnel or facilities is likely.” There was no immediate confirmation of the threat level at RAF Fairford. U.S. European Command said it would not discuss “specific force protection measures.” © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. 30 remarkable women to watch in Ireland’s STEM space
2026-09-25

30 remarkable women to watch in Ireland’s STEM space

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
If you want something to inspire your own professional ambitions, consider taking a closer look at the careers of these 30 impressive women in STEM. Whether you are a student, a graduate, an established professional or at the retirement stage of your life, many STEM professionals past and present enjoy keeping up to date with the latest news, advancements and research. An ideal way to do just that is to ensure that you are following the careers of the people who are right at the centre of innovation and exploration in the STEM space. With that in mind, as part of SiliconRepublic.com’s STEM career coverage, we have compiled a list of some of the industries most influential women. If you are looking for a role model to follow, who is active in the AI space, then look no further than Prof Abeba Birhane. Birhane is the founder and a principal investigator at the Artificial Intelligence Accountability Lab at Trinity College Dublin, as well as an assistant professor of AI in the School of Computer Science and Statistics. She was previously a senior fellow in trustworthy AI at Mozilla. Her primary area of expertise is in cognitive AI, particularly the ethics and the long-term, societal implications of the technology. She also served on the United Nations Secretary-General AI Advisory Body and the AI Advisory Council in Ireland. Birhane’s work has resulted in her inclusion on a number of high-profile lists recognising her among the leaders of the AI space and she has received several awards, including a mention on the Time 100 most influential persons in AI 2023 list, and the Distinguished Paper Award 2024 at the IEEE Conference on Secure and Trustworthy Machine Learning. For more than 20 years, Aisling Shannon has worked at the European Space Agency (ESA), first as a structures engineer and more recently as a senior programme adviser. Over the course of her career she has been responsible for Earth observation spacecraft development, covering design, test and launcher interface, as well as a range of non-technical elements that accompany developments in this field. In her role as a senior programme adviser, she is involved in independent assessments and reviews of ESA activities. Eager to give back to others in the community, Shannon has previously served as a UNOOSA Space4Women mentor and a Talent Girl mentor, hoping to inspire and guide other women and girls. Aleksandra Kaszubowska is an E3 assistant professor in electrical and electronic engineering at Trinity College Dublin. She is also a senior research fellow for leading Irish research organisation the Connect Centre and she previously participated in a deep-tech pre-accelerator programme at the Tyndall National Institute. At the Connect Centre, she is working at the intersection of a number of different areas, including optical networks, hardware, controls and network layers. Kaszubowska has more than 15 years of experience in the field of photonic communications gained through work in both industrial and academic research environments. Her specialties include end-to-end optical transmission system design and modelling, hybrid radio-over-fibre systems, optical packet switched networks, advanced modulation formats and coherent detection. She received the 2025 Women in STEM Award for Engineering for her work in the engineering sector and has engaged with the media to share her ideas and knowledge with the wider STEM community. Aoibhinn Ní Shúilleabháin is a well-known figure in the STEM industry and for good reason. She is an associate professor of mathematics education at the DCU School of STEM Education, Innovation and Global Studies and she has a wealth of experience working on policy in education, higher education, research and the environment. She is deeply concerned with broadening STEM education and promoting gender equality. She was previously the chair of the Independent Advisory Committee on Nature Restoration, she is the former chair of Ireland’s national Citizens’ Assembly on Biodiversity Loss and she is an experienced and skilled broadcaster, public speaker, discussion moderator, workshop facilitator and science communicator. One such workshop currently being led by Ní Shúilleabháin is the Maths Sparks Problem Solving Workshops, which is a series of extra-curricular mathematics workshops for senior cycle post-primary school students. She has spoken at conferences and has been a part of the discussion at events held by organisations such as the Department of Foreign Affairs, European Movement Ireland, National Cyber Security Centre, and the Royal Irish Academy and Teaching Council. In 2021, Prof Caitriona Jackman became the first woman senior professor of physics at the Dublin Institute of Advanced Studies (DIAS) and since her appointment has been a trail blazer in planetary science. As the head of the DIAS Planetary Magnetospheres group, she has vast experience with data analysis, theoretical modelling and mission planning. She also has specialist skills and knowledge in the areas of the dynamics of planetary magnetospheres and solar wind influence, machine learning, data analytics, processing pipelines, outreach and public engagement. She has previously served on the Science and Technology Facilities Council, the Solar System Advisory Panel, the Royal Astronomical Society Council, and the European Space Agency Solar System and Exploration Working Group. Workday’s CTO for EMEA Clare Hickie has built a long and impactful career within the STEM space. One of the areas she is focused on is staying competitive in the age of artificial intelligence. Having spent a number of years working across a range of industries, such as pharmaceuticals, biotechnology and IT HR, Hickie thrives off of embracing change and the transformation of the STEM space. She was also recently included in Computer Weekly’s 2026 Most Influential Women in UK Tech longlist. Deirdre MacCarthy is the chief legal officer and head of people at fintech Wayflyer. She previously advised the company during a €7.8m seed funding round and through her role at Wayflyer has worked with a number of dynamic companies in Ireland’s STEM space, such as Stripe. Last year, as part of the Wayflyer delegate, she travelled to the US to strengthen Irish-US business connections and celebrated the contributions of Irish Americans at the Ireland Funds Gala event. Dr Deirdre Murray is a consultant paediatrician and senior lecturer in the Department of Paediatrics and Child Health at University College Cork. She is also the principal investigator of the Cork Baseline birth cohort, which is reportedly one of the largest and most detailed mother-child cohorts in Europe and the first and currently only longitudinal birth cohort in Ireland. Her research is focused on early brain injury and the development of new ways to predict and assess neuro-cognitive outcomes in children. She was previously awarded a prestigious Health Research Board Clinician Scientist Award for BiHiVE, which is an ongoing project focusing on the discovery and validation of biomarkers in neonatal brain injury. In 2025, she won the Role Model of the Year Award at the Women in STEM awards. Engineer Dervilla Mitchell is a co-chair at the Royal Academy of Engineering, an industry advisory board member at Equatic.tech and previously worked at Arup. She has experience working on both small- and large-scale projects, such as work at Portcullis House in Westminster, Saga HQ, Kent, Heathrow Terminal 5, the Abu Dhabi Midfield Terminal project, the Athletes’ Village at the London 2012 Olympics and the Goodwood Paddock Redevelopment. She is committed to strengthening the diversity of the engineering landscape and works to champion equality. She also urges young people to consider contributing their insights, knowledge and creativity to work that can impact the current climate crisis. Mitchell works with the National Engineering Policy Centre and the Professional Engineering Institutions to better understand and relay the challenges of decarbonisation. The vice-president of research and innovation at Technological University Dublin, Dr Dympna O’Sullivan’s primary research interest is in human-centred artificial intelligence, with a focus on designing AI-driven assistive technologies to support independent living, particularly for people with age-related cognitive impairments. She is an advocate for greater public and patient involvement in research that focuses on the lived experience of those who are frequently marginalised in the software development process. She regularly adds to the conversation on computing ethics and responsible AI, has contributed to the development of a European curricula for digital ethics and she frequently collaborates with national TV, radio and print media outlets to share her thoughts on AI. She also takes part in educational and public engagement activities such as talks, panel discussions and workshops designed to make AI more accessible to the general public. Elaine Coughlan is a managing partner and co-founder of Atlantic Bridge, a deep-tech investment fund that has offices in Dublin, London, Munich, Paris and Palo Alto. She has more than 25 years of experience scaling and growing companies and was previously the founder and director of GloNav. She is also an investment committee member at British Business Bank and holds board and adviser positions at the King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, Irish Life Health, Consello, Endeavour Ireland and Scale Ireland. Coughlan was named among Forbes 50, Inspiring Top 50 Women in Europe and Europe’s Top 100 Women in Technology. Dr Fidelma Dunne is a professor of medicine at the University of Galway, a consultant endocrinologist at University Hospital Galway, an associate director at the Clinical Research Facility, Galway and the former president of the Irish Endocrine Society. Her main area of focus is pregnancy and diabetes and she has written more than 270 peer-reviewed publications. She has been involved in a number of international studies and was the Irish lead for a pan-European cohort study examining pregnancy outcomes for women with Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes and a study examining insulin Degludec in women with Type 1 Diabetes in pregnancy. She was the PI of the recently funded Health Research Board All Ireland Collaborative Clinical Trial Network in Diabetes 2021-2026. Prof Ines Thiele is the principal investigator of the Molecular Systems Physiology group and Director of the Digital Metabolic Twin Centre at the University of Galway. She is also a principal investigator at APC Microbiome Ireland and a co-principal investigator on Theme 2, Diet and Microbes. Her research aims to improve the understanding of how diet influences human health and to do that she uses a computational modelling approach, termed constraint-based modelling, which is reportedly increasing in importance for systems biology. In 2013, she received the ATTRACT fellowship from the Fonds National de la Recherche in Luxembourg; in 2015, she was elected as the EMBO Young Investigator; in 2017, she was awarded an ERC starting grant; and in 2018, she was an awardee of the NUI Galway Foundation Research Leadership Programme. Jacky Fox has led Accenture’s global secure digital core practice since April of this year, having held various other leadership positions at the organisation since joining as the Ireland lead and global CTO of security in 2019. She is also the vice-chair of the board for Cyber Ireland and previously held other senior positions at Dell, Deloitte and University College Dublin. In 2018, Fox was recognised as Security Champion of the Year and she has taken part in a number of key industry events and panels, including at the Global Cybersecurity Forum in Riyadh and at the World Economic Forum’s annual meeting on cybersecurity. The head of EMEA for fintech Fiserv, Katia Karpova has previous experience as an executive director for JP Morgan and was also a programme manager for the Institute for Public Private Partnerships. In her role at the organisation, Karpova oversees Fiserv’s activities in at least 13 countries, across issuing, payments software, merchant acquiring and processing services for financial institutions. She holds a master’s degree in business administration from Stanford University, a bachelor’s degree in finance from University of Maryland and is fluent in Ukrainian, Russian and English. The most actionable data to drive superior results She is also regularly asked to comment on topics and advancements in the ever-changing finance landscape. In June, she was a guest speaker at the Money 20/20 event and was also featured on SiliconRepublic’s own The Leaders’ Room podcast series. Lavinia Morris is the general manager for EMEA data centre operations at Microsoft. She has extensive experience across a multitude of industries, such as banking, aviation, insurance and professional services. Her particular area of expertise is in digital transformation, innovation, strategic execution and operational management. Morris’ previous roles include COO of KBC Bank Ireland, director of the Irish Banking Culture board, director of Synch Payments DAC and the chief information officer at SMBC Aviation Capital. Currently, her priorities include scaling innovation in critical infrastructure, delivering resilient, secure cloud operations, enabling AI and digital sovereignty and championing diversity, equity and STEAM, to improve the wider ecosystem. Maria O’Dwyer is the infrastructure delivery director for Uisce Éireann, where she is largely responsible for the delivery of its water and wastewater capital investment programme. She is a qualified chartered engineer, has a degree in electronic engineering and a master’s in engineering science, as well as various diplomas in supply chain management, business, coaching and company direction. Her current work is broad, covering all activities from design, planning and procurement through to construction delivery. She is also a non-executive director for the Sustainable Energy Authority of Ireland. Her recent projects include the high-profile €114m Athlone main drainage scheme and the Water Supply Project Eastern and Midlands Region. Much of O’Dwyer’s work focuses on bolstering infrastructure, making water supplies more resilient and managing waste water more effectively. Making waves in the regulatory affairs space, Muriel O’Byrne is the senior vice-president for international regulatory affairs and the European business office at pharmaceutical company Regeneron. She was previously the senior director for global regulatory affairs at Elan Pharmaceuticals, the director for EU regulatory affairs at AstraZeneca and senior regulatory executive at GSK. She has been featured in EUCOPE, which is the European Confederation of Pharmaceutical Entrepreneurs, and also contributed to a World Clinical Trials Day 2026 feature in EMJ Gold, where she explored regulatory challenges in biologics, genetics and precision medicine. Engineer, scientist, explorer and science communicator Dr Niamh Shaw is a well-known figure in the STEM space. She is renowned for her reports covering major space missions, where she translates the mindset of astronauts into insight for leaders navigating uncertainty, complexity and long-term strategy. She was previously an analogue astronaut at the Mars Desert Research Station, has trained in extreme environments from Antarctica and the Arctic Circle to zero-gravity flight, worked in the Kalahari and Atacama deserts, and has reported from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center and the European Space Agency. She is also a highly requested keynote speaker and has shared her knowledge on space, leadership, curiosity and exploration at a range of high-profile corporate events, conferences and festivals all over the world. She is the CEO and founder of Dream Big Space and Science Communications and is an advisory board member for the AstroAid Foundation. Dr Rathnait Long is a distinguished fellow of technology at semiconductor company Macom. Her main area of focus and research is in the development of semiconductor materials and devices. She is also a technical reviewer for the National Science Foundation and for more than seven years was an adjunct professor at Stanford University. An innovator in the semiconductor industry, Long has significant experience in the patent application process, including for acoustic resonator manufacturing methods and printed circuit board design patents, which were filed on behalf of Macom. She also regularly contributes to the wider space via articles and journals and recently took part in SiliconRepublic.com’s The Leaders’ Room podcast series, where she discussed her career. Dr Ruth Freeman is the director of research for society at Research Ireland, where she oversees individually led research programmes, centres for research training, challenge-based research programmes, and the education and public engagement brief. Prior to her current role at Research Ireland, she filled a number of roles at Science Foundation Ireland and during her time at Trinity College Dublin she earned a scholarship, the Eli Lilly Chemistry Prize and the Roberts Prize for Biology. Freeman is vocal about the importance of inclusion, climate justice and the power of education, technology and innovation to transform lives. She is of the opinion that all voices need to be heard in the conversation around STEM and she has lent hers to the topic several times. Dr Sarah Bourke is the CEO and co-founder of Skytek, a software company that innovates for a number of demanding industries, including the space sector. She has been recognised among her peers and industry professionals for her leadership and success in the commercialisation of space technology, as shown when she won third place of the European Prize for Women Innovators in 2016. She frequently works alongside professionals from high-profile institutions such as the European Space Agency and NASA, as she further develops Skytek. Prior to founding Skytek, she worked at a number of leading universities and governmental agencies. The CEO and co-founder of Shorla Oncology, Sharon Cunningham is leading the charge when it comes to meeting unmet needs in the global pharmaceutical space. Her role involves the development and commercialisation of critical oncology products to assist with patient care. She is a board member at US-based life science nonprofit MassBio, Blood Cancer United, the Healthcare Business Network and Cork University Business School. Healthtech Ireland CEO and consultant Susan Treacy has more than 25 years of healthcare experience nationally and internationally. Silicon Republic
Ireland losing out on future female talent with early workplace exits
2026-09-25

Ireland losing out on future female talent with early workplace exits

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
New research has highlighted how gender-related challenges may be limiting women’s potential for leadership roles in the workplace. Balance for Better Business (BFBB), a review group for greater equality in the workplace, has today (25 September) published the results of a report exploring how an early exit from the workforce is more common among women than men, often to the detriment of female leadership and the wider ecosystem. BFBB’s research found that women aged 50 to 65 are more likely to leave the workforce at an earlier age than their male counterparts, reducing the pool of experienced talent available for senior leadership and board roles. The ‘Why Women Exit the Workforce Earlier than Men in Later Career Stages: Irish and International Perspective and Policy Recommendations’ report found that a number of issues are arising that are contributing to a rise in women exiting the workforce early. Despite Irish women showing higher than average workplace participation rates, the research found that Ireland also has a larger participation gap between men and women aged 55 to 65, when compared to EU figures. According to the report: “This gap is particularly significant around the 50-65 age cohort, as it coincides with the period when many professionals are reaching senior executive and board-level positions. As experienced women leave the workforce at greater rates than men, organisations risk losing valuable talent and future leadership candidates.” Among the challenges were menopause, caregiving responsibilities, burnout, workplace ageism and barriers to career progression. The report suggested that there is an opportunity for women to be better supported in the workplace provided there is greater recognition of how the burden of caregiving often falls on women. BFBB found that women account for 61pc of unpaid carers in Ireland, with many women, at a later stage of their careers, balancing working alongside caring for children or ageing relatives. The report said: “Employers should strengthen support for carers through flexible working arrangements, enhanced workplace support and policies that encourage a more balanced sharing of caregiving responsibilities.” Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Organisations should also pay greater consideration to the impact of ageism and gender bias in the workplace, as women often find their progression to be limited, increasing the likelihood of a workforce exit. Findings from the report indicated that more than 90pc of workers aged over 55 have experienced age-related discrimination. “Organisations should strengthen inclusive leadership practices, address bias in recruitment and promotion processes, and ensure experienced women remain visible, valued and supported to progress.” The menopause is also creating additional challenges for women in the workplace, with the report regarding menopause as a “significant but often overlooked factor in workforce participation”. More than one-third of women who contributed their data to the research said that they have considered leaving work due to menopause symptoms, while an additional 9.2pc have already left employment as a result. The report said: “Employers should introduce menopause policies, manager training and targeted workplace support to help retain experienced female talent and prevent avoidable exits from the workforce.” Commenting on the results of the report, Carolan Lennon, the co-chair of Balance for Better Business, said: “This report shines a spotlight on an important but often overlooked challenge. Women in their 50s and early 60s represent a highly experienced and skilled segment of Ireland’s workforce, yet too many continue to encounter barriers that make remaining in employment unnecessarily difficult. “Retaining experienced women must be a strategic priority. Doing so is not only essential to achieving balanced leadership, but also to strengthening the competitiveness and future growth of Irish businesses. If we want to achieve balanced leadership, we must ensure that women can thrive and progress throughout every stage of their careers.” Bernard Byrne, also co-chair of Balance for Better Business, added: “The earlier exit of so many women from the workforce has implications far beyond individual organisations. It directly impacts the pools of talent needed to have broad and diverse leadership pipelines. “This limitation has a direct knock-on impact on the competitiveness of Irish business. The actions identified in this report are practical and can help employers to make a real difference in retaining a balanced talent pool. Businesses that take action today will be better positioned to attract, retain and develop the diverse leadership talent of the future.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. ASML sold nothing in Europe this past quarter

ASML sold nothing in Europe this past quarter

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
ASML Headquarters, Veldhoven. Image: © Bart van Overbeeke, 2018 The chip equipment maker’s European sale share dropped to nothing from a measly 1pc. The Dutch chip industry keystone ASML sold nothing to European customers this past quarter, in what should be a worrying sign for the region as it attempts to catch up with the US and China, a senior executive for the company said in a panel earlier this week. Europe should spend its time developing its capital markets and removing red tape, rather than on foreign competition, ASML’s executive vice-president Frank Heemskerk told the panel. He added that “from factory to chip design to cloud computing, very little happens here”. This comes more than a year after an EU special report found that the region’s Chips Act was not enough to reach its Digital Decade goal, which targeted a 20pc share in the global semiconductor market by 2030, despite “reasonable progress” in implementing it. The April 2025 report said that the “overly ambitious” target would be hard to reach given the European Commission’s limited mandate and resources, reliance on member state’s actions, private sector investments and other factors such as energy costs. It recommended that the EU to carry out an “urgent reality check” and begin preparing the next strategy. ASML plays an extremely important role in the intricately connected global semiconductor supply chain, as it is the only company that makes the sophisticated lithography equipment key to producing advanced chips. Its machines are used by the biggest chip manufacturers globally, including the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company and Samsung, to print minuscule transistors widely used across electronic devices. ASML’s European sale share dropped to nothing from a measly 1pc, which, according to PwC’s chief economist for The Netherlands region Barbara Baarsma, is a clear demand issue. Baarsma, on the panel, pointed to how the French domestic intelligence service ditched Palantir in favour of domestic AI providers earlier this year, and said that governments should act as launching customers to bundle demand. The chipmaking machine provider’s biggest market is South Korea, which represented 43pc of ASML’s Q2 sales, followed by Taiwan, China, the US and Japan. The company expects full-year sales to come in between €43bn and €45bn. What’s important for ASML is that Europe catches up, Heemskerk said, adding that the equipment maker is getting courted to expand its presence in the US, China and India. Google plans satellite launch to test AI compute in space
2026-09-25

Google plans satellite launch to test AI compute in space

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
The organisation will launch a prototype satellite as a first in-orbit test of Project Suncatcher. Google has confirmed plans to launch a prototype satellite next week as part of its strategy to study how AI computing infrastructure operates in space. As part of the test, Google will deploy its tensor processing units (TPUs) in space and evaluate how they perform. The first in-orbit test is a part of Project Suncatcher, which is a research effort designed to explore space’s potential to host large-scale AI computing infrastructure, particularly under harsh conditions. In a blogpost, Google’s senior director for its Paradigms of Intelligence team Travis Beals said: “Project Suncatcher is a long-term, research moonshot exploring whether space could one day host scalable machine learning infrastructure. “In low Earth orbit, satellites can access near-constant sunlight, generating up to eight times more solar power than on Earth. Eventually, it could be possible to link together multiple constellations of satellites, allowing them to manage larger AI workloads while in orbit.” Beals added: “Turning that idea into reality starts with a basic question, can our AI hardware operate in space? This initial mission onboard the upcoming Transporter-18 rideshare mission with SpaceX was developed in partnership with Planet. “It’s designed to gather in-orbit data on how our TPUs handle the physical stress of spaceflight and the radiation and thermal extremes of space.” According to Google, a rocket trip into low orbit takes roughly 10 minutes, during which the craft experiences intense vibrations and sustained acceleration that can be 10 times the force of gravity (g-force). Beals said: “Individual components, such as the TPU chips, can experience even greater forces up to 50 to 100 g. The team conducted vibration testing by intensely shaking the satellite on all three axes to mimic the frequencies of a rocket launch.” Once in space, the TPUs will then be exposed to radiation outside of the Earth’s atmosphere, as well as solar events and cosmic rays that can disrupt electronics. Google’s TPUs were tested in a proton beam facility at UC Davis’s Crocker Nuclear Laboratory while running AI workloads. Beals said: “During the test, we monitored closely to see how errors, like a bitflip, would affect our workloads. Initial results have shown that our Trillium TPUs hold up remarkably well and can survive a radiation total ionising dose greater than what they would receive during a five-year space mission.” Tests, while useful, are only half the equation, as noted by Beals who explained that the real test is in how the TPUs perform in space. The hope is that, by putting the TPUs into orbit, the team will be able to gather data and learnings to inform future launches. “Exploring space as a viable location for scalable AI compute won’t happen all at once,” said Beals. “It takes methodical engineering, starting with proving our hardware can handle the physical and unpredictable realities of operating in orbit. This first launch is about seeing what works, identifying points of failure, and applying those findings to future missions.” Earlier this month, Google announced its largest single investment in Europe to date with a commitment to invest €13bn into Finland’s digital infrastructure over the course of the next two years. This will include supporting data centre build-outs, and the funding of clean energy and nature restoration projects. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Databricks buys Row Zero to bring governed spreadsheets to Genie

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-25

Visit Original Website
Ali Ghodsi, co-founder and CEO of Databricks. Image: Databricks Databricks has acquired Row Zero, bringing a governed, live-data spreadsheet into Genie just weeks after the company’s valuation hit $190bn. San Francisco’s Databricks has acquired Row Zero, a spreadsheet tool built to connect business teams’ spreadsheets directly to live, governed data in a move to expand the capabilities of Genie, its AI coworker product. No financial details have been disclosed. The deal will give Genie a native spreadsheet interface, letting users explore data, model scenarios and collaborate directly inside a familiar, Excel- and Google Sheets-like environment. Unlike a typical spreadsheet, Row Zero – which was founded by former AWS and Tableau engineers – connects to live, governed data sources, with queries honouring each user’s permissions and every interaction auditable, according to Databricks. The company said the acquisition addresses what it called “spreadmarts” – ungoverned spreadsheet files that teams have long relied on to store or share data – which it argues create security and governance gaps as businesses deploy AI agents on sensitive company data. “As more finance, operations, and go-to-market teams adopt Genie, incorporating a seamless spreadsheet experience is a must-have,” said Patrick Wendell, co-founder and VP of engineering at Databricks. “When finance started doing all their work with Genie, we noticed that they were combining Genie with a Live Cloud Spreadsheet called Row Zero,” Databricks CEO and co-founder Ali Ghodsi said in a social media post. “This combination is really powerful. We met the Row Zero team and were blown away.” Row Zero founder and CEO Breck Fresen said the team built the product because it wanted spreadsheets that could “seamlessly connect to enterprise-scale data with governance and security at the core”. Row Zero will be available to Databricks customers across all major clouds and will continue to support data sources beyond Databricks’ own platform, the company said. The acquisition follows a period of rapid growth for Databricks. The company closed a $5bn funding round in August at a $190bn valuation, up from $134bn just six months earlier, with backers including Coatue, Blackstone and MGX. Founded in 2013, Databricks works with more than 20,000 organisations, including Adidas, AT&T, Bayer, Block, Mastercard and Unilever, and employs more than 500 staff across the UK and Ireland as part of its EMEA operations, headquartered in London. The company’s quantum systems are used across sectors by customers including Saudi Aramco, Crédit Agricole CIB and LG Electronics, and is supported by partnerships with Nvidia and IBM. Pasqal has additional offices in Saudi Arabia, Canada, the US and South Korea. Recent milestones, including the demonstration of more than 1,000 physical qubits, industry-leading progress in logical qubits, and the successful application of our systems to solve complex differential equations and simulate materials beyond the practical reach of classical computing, reinforce our confidence in the strength and differentiation of Pasqal’s neutral-atom approach. “With a growing portfolio of customer engagements, a differentiated technology platform, a disciplined approach to capital allocation, and a strong balance sheet to support future growth, we remain focused on delivering practical quantum solutions that create value today while building the foundation for the next generation of quantum computing,” he said. For the past few weeks, Elon Musk has been throwing a tantrum — and throwing around legal threats — over Alex Gibney’s “hit piece” documentary about him that’s set to release next month. Now, as if following his lead, pretty much all of the major social media sites are refusing to run ads for the movie, according to The Hollywood Reporter. The platforms — YouTube, TikTok, Instagram, and Facebook — are unwilling to accept paid advertising for the film. And of course so is X, the site owned by Musk himself. The stonewalling has reportedly taken distributor Bleecker Street by surprise, with the company scrambling to figure out why its ads have been so widely rejected. YouTube, TikTok, and Facebook and Instagram owner Meta have all provided a reason related to “political content” to the distributor for why they turned down the ad, per the reporting. It’s an even stranger argument for YouTube, which has no prohibitions on political advertising. If, as what appears to be happening, the tech industry is closing ranks around one of its own, it would be an astonishing testament to Musk’s influence and would represent an ominous threat to free speech. The film requires advertising to reach an audience, and if major social media platforms can collude to ice out works that criticize figures in their industry, it makes it so that there’s not only less of an audience seeing these critiques, but that producing these critical works is financially unsustainable. The nearly four-hour film, titled “Musk,” is a comprehensive and critical look at the billionaire’s life, beginning with his upbringing in South Africa and running through his success as a tech entrepreneur and his rise as a major and polarizing political figure. Musk has been raging against the film since it was first announced in 2023, calling it a “hit piece,” even though there were barely any details about it at the time. In the past month, he’s ramped up his attacks against the documentary, calling Gibney a “douchebag” and accusing him of being “incredibly biased.” He also, via his lawyer Alex Spiro, threatened to sue Gibney’s production company Jigsaw Productions for defamation. This claim centers on how the documentary supposedly promotes a debunked conspiracy theory that Musk used his Starlink satellites to help Donald Trump secure votes in the 2024 presidential election. Bleecker Street has appealed to the social media sites over the rejected trailer ads. YouTube and TikTok are still considering the appeal, while Meta, which owns Facebook and Instagram, has already rejected them. Musk has a lengthy track record of silencing his critics. After taking over X, formerly Twitter, he spuriously banned journalists that were critical of him, and he’s often resorted to using hush money to keep a bad word from being spoken about him. AI Bros Are Having a Meltdown Because the Associated Press Stylebook Clarifies That AIs Don’t Have Feelings

AI Bros Are Having a Meltdown Because the Associated Press Stylebook Clarifies That AIs Don’t Have Feelings

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
In the midst of heated discussions on AI safety and consciousness, the Associated Press Stylebook has reminded everyone of where it stands on the whole “anthropomorphism” debate. “Artificial intelligence systems do not think, feel, want or understand. Avoid language that gives them human characteristics,” it wrote Wednesday in a post on X. “This is called anthropomorphizing, when we ascribe human traits, emotions or behaviors to non-human things, such as animals or inanimate objects.” This didn’t go over well in the world of AI experts and adherents, whose nerves, shall we say, were collectively struck by what they characterized as a “completely insane” declaration. “I’m not sure a *style book* should be making such confident metaphysical pronouncements,” fumed Alan Rozenshtein, a member of technical staff at Anthropic. Henry Shevlin, a philosopher and AI ethicist at Google DeepMind, suggested that the AP was being hypocritical because it anthropomorphized none other than Optimus Prime — the fictional, sapient robot from the “Transformers” franchise — in a film review. “An AP movie review can tell us Optimus Prime mourns and loves, but readers need protecting from ‘the computer understands,'” he wrote. “We routinely use mentalistic language without literal commitment. You’d hope a style guide could distinguish using words from believing things.” OpenAI’s head of strategic futures Dean Ball was also indignant. “AIs *clearly* think and understand,” he averred. “If your definition of ‘think’ excludes entities that can autonomously resolve Millennium Problems, it is your definition that is flawed,” he added, referring to how OpenAI’s models found a solution to a famous math problem (a claim that itself is fraught with controversy). “This desire to avoid anthropomorphic language has become a pathology,” he added. It’s true that there’s some debate among philosophers and scientists over whether AI models think or feel, but even saying that’s probably lending the AI-is-sentient-camp a little too much credence. Fascinating a possibility as it is to consider, there’s no consensus that AI systems have these experiences. It’s a testament to how defensive, if not cultish, that AI advocates have become that merely suggesting that large language models don’t think, let alone in the way humans do, is cause for indignation. Even supposing that it’s possible that AIs are already experiencing human-like emotions, why not err on the side of caution and stick to language that doesn’t bias us in favor of viewing these things as already alive, especially when there’s a trillion dollar industry at work trying to hype its tech as a powerful force that we should let change our lives? Futurism
Venus May Have Devoured Its Moon, Scientists Find
2026-09-27

Venus May Have Devoured Its Moon, Scientists Find

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Venus is often called the Earth’s “evil twin” since they’re roughly the same size, formed in the same inner part of the solar system, and happen to be neighbors as well — although, as suggested by that moniker, Venus is horrendously inhospitable to life as we know it compared to our home planet. The similarities may run even deeper than previously known. Scientists have previously suggested that Venus once had a moon orbiting it, which met its demise when an enormous object impacted with it. Other researchers believe its current lack of moons — beyond several trojan asteroids that don’t directly orbit the planet and therefore don’t meet the definition of a moon — means that Venus never experienced the kind of impact that could’ve dislodged enough material to form a natural satellite. Now, a new study published in The Astrophysical Journal upends that consensus, with the authors suggesting that Venus did indeed once have a moon — until it spiraled inwards and crashed into the planet’s surface in an apocalyptic demise. Put simply, Venus may have devoured its own moon. “My study shows Venus didn’t require a catastrophe to arrive at what we can see today,” said lead author and University of California, Riverside, astrophysicist Stephen Kane in a statement. “It turns out the gravity of the planet itself combined with the rate at which it spins naturally caused the moon to collapse on top of it.” The findings could have significant implications, even when it comes to investigating the habitability of distant worlds. “When people think about Earth twins around other stars, one question they ask is, ‘Does it have a moon?'” Kane said. “”My study shows a disturbing scenario for many of those cases. If these planets don’t rotate fast enough, the moon will crash to the surface, and that would change the course of history for those planets.” Moons don’t always have a perfectly stable orbit. We know that our own Moon is moving away from us at roughly two inches a year, the result of the Earth’s relatively fast spin. Venus, on the other hand, takes far longer than 24 hours to complete a rotation, completing one revolution every 243 Earth days. That sluggish spin means that a hypothetical moon may not have enough energy to escape Venus’ graviational pull, and would be doomed to collide with its surface eventually. Kane and his team used computer simulations to simulate this behavior, based on the interaction between the Earth and the Moon and consistently found that the moon would succumb to Venus’ slow rotation. “When I made this discovery, I was shocked,” Kane said in the statement. “I thought surely the broad range of scenarios I was exploring would lead to a variety of results. But it all went pretty much in the same direction.” It’s important to note that the paper doesn’t necessarily mean that Venus had a moon in the first place — but if it did, it likely was devoured by the planet. Finding direct evidence about the existence of a past moon is extremely difficult. Some 80 percent of Venus’ surface dates back to around a billion years ago, indicating that its geological record may have been wiped out during a major resurfacing event. Other possibilities include material of a possible moon buried deep beneath Venus’ surface, something that would require extensive research to investigate. Intriguingly, scientists suggest a major collision that could’ve formed a Venuvian moon could’ve made its once-habitable surface no longer capable of supporting life, turning it into the extremely hot and inhospitable place it’s known to be today. At the same time, researchers still debate whether a moon is even necessary at all for life to flourish. “My feeling is there are benefits to having a moon, but it isn’t required for habitability,” Kane explained in his statement. “The moon has definitely changed the way Earth has evolved through time, but we don’t fully know how important that role is.” More on Venus: Scientists Detect Weird Anomalies in Clouds of Venus
Futurism
Workers Are Spending An Astonishing Amount of Their Own Money On AI for Their Jobs
2026-09-27

Workers Are Spending An Astonishing Amount of Their Own Money On AI for Their Jobs

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
While a rising majority of workers in the take a negative attitude toward AI in the workplace, others are welcoming the tech with open arms — even going so far as to pay for AI services out of their own pockets. According to a recent workplace survey conducted by Deloitte and flagged by Reuters, workers in the UK are spending a collective $1.3 billion annually on generative AI for their jobs, without even expensing it. The survey is based on responses from 25,000 workers across the continent, which Deloitte calls the “largest study of workplace GenAI use conducted in a single country.” Overall, the AI adoption rate in UK workplaces is massive, with 63 percent of working adults reporting that they actively use generative AI for work. Though the polling methods aren’t an exact match, we can compare that to a similarly-timed survey of US workers by Gallup, which suggests that 52 percent of American workers use AI in some capacity, though only 15 percent do so daily. In the UK, nearly half of the workers surveyed said they use free AI tools, while 34 percent said they used generative AI provided by their employers. Still, one in six workplace AI users reported paying for at least one kind of tool themselves — the cohort collectively spending over $1 billion on AI every year. Despite their obvious enthusiasm for the technology, the Deloitte survey found UK workers’ actual deployment of generative AI remains pretty shallow. Rather than chasing cutting-edge autonomous agents — which are not always the most efficient choice, to be fair — British workers are using AI for surface-level tasks like pumping out emails, or as a replacement for once-reliable search engines (a method one CEO likened to lobbing “slop grenades” around the office.) As partner and head of industry insight at Deloitte UK Paul Lee said in the report, “what’s particularly striking is how quickly GenAI has become part of everyday working life. Yet for many workers its role remains relatively basic, focused on searching for information and drafting content rather than supporting more complex work.” “The story here isn’t that workers are using GenAI,” Lee concluded, but that “they are using it despite limited training, patchy guidance and, in some cases, without their employer’s knowledge.” More on AI: Support for Unions Is Spiking as AI Threatens Jobs

This will be the first one-on-one meeting between the two men, who recently found themselves on opposite sides of the AI safety debate. Amodei released a plan to slow AI development (or at least proceed with more caution), while Trump has insisted, without evidence, that the AI backlash is a Democratic hoax; he also wants to rebrand the technology as “super intelligence.” Even before the current back-and-forth, Amodei and Anthropic have to had a fraught relationship with Trump’s administration. Earlier this year, the Pentagon designated Anthropic a supply-chain risk in response to the company’s attempt to put guardrails around the use of its technology (Anthropic has been fighting the designation in court), although other administration officials have been friendlier. Can Muse overcome Meta’s trust issues?

Can Muse overcome Meta’s trust issues?

TechCrunch | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Meta’s new AI agent Muse took the spotlight at the company’s annual Connect event, where CEO Mark Zuckerberg made it clear that Facebook’s parent company plans to push AI features everywhere. On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Kirsten Korosec, Sean O’Kane, and I discussed Meta’s AI announcements seemed to steal the spotlight during a week of new model launches from OpenAI and Anthropic. With other big AI companies focused on coding and enterprise tools, it was a little surprising to see Meta move in the opposite direction, with a consumer focus and a cute, Tamagotchi-style AI device that Meta insists is for adults only. But as Kirsten noted, this could be playing to Meta’s strengths. Sean tried Muse for himself, and while he was pleased that the agent actually found him some unclaimed money, he described the feature as more “a party-trick type thing,” rather than something that will drive ongoing usage. Plus, there’s the question of whether users can trust Meta’s AI with sensitive information. “Meta’s business is to sell you ads,” Sean said. “And yes, they’ll make the argument that the more they know about you, the more accurate and interesting the ads will be — wake me up when we get to that fever dream.” Keep reading for a preview of our full conversation, edited for length and clarity. Kirsten Korosec: So how do you put Muse, which is this new personal AI agent that’s just been released by Meta and [is] clearly a bet on consumer — how does that fit into what you just described, at least with other frontier AI model companies seeing opportunity and business within enterprise? Because Meta Connect, which is their big annual event, just happened, and they are all-in on Muse, that is very clear. Anthony Ha: That was definitely very head spinning for me, because it certainly feels like what we’ve been talking about has been this shift towards enterprise — not exclusively, but certainly that’s where the money, the attention is going. Maybe some of that is because of the relative position of these different companies — OpenAI and Anthropic are in the lead in a lot of ways, but also, they’re planning to go public either this year, or next year in the case of OpenAI. And so there’s this feeling of, “I think we’ve got to actually make money now.” Not to say that they’re not making money [already], but because the costs are so high and the valuations are so high, they have to make money on this scale that’s essentially unprecedented. And I think they’re seeing enterprise as the way to do that. And I wonder if Meta, for a variety of reasons, sees a different opportunity. There’s a part of me that’s like, “Wait, did they not get the memo?” But I think more charitably, you could say, “Well, if that’s where OpenAI and Anthropic are going, then maybe there is more of an opportunity for Meta to make the more consumer-friendly [version and] continue advancing AI on the consumer side.” Kirsten: I mean, we can complain about or criticize or critique Meta all day long, but they’re very good and have [an] established track record of embedding themselves in everyday people’s lives. I mean, there’s a reason why Facebook has so many users — Instagram, WhatsApp. And I’ve never really thought of them as an enterprise product anyway. So I think it’s smart for them to continue to push on the consumer piece. Sean, you’ve already tried Muse, which has already been out for a couple weeks. And I’m wondering if you see what your impression is, and if you see it being successful in the bid to become part of every part of your life. Sean O’Kane: I mean, not really. I understand why some people think that is going to be the case. I’m sure a lot of people understand this, but this is roughly Meta’s kind ground-up version of an on-your iPhone, or on your Android, app of OpenClaw, which we talked about a couple months ago, which Meta went out and basically bought and integrated those folks’ work. It was the first big explosion of like, “Holy smokes, these agents can do all this stuff for me while I’m out and about, and I can just text with it and let it control my whole computer.” There’s a lot of the same elements of that at play. And having it in your hand, on an app that works like a relatively good chatbot as the interface, it does seem pretty powerful. One of the first things that I did with it was — because it makes a bunch of suggestions for you, as to things that it can do, and one of them was, “I’ll scan to see if you have any unclaimed funds,” this thing that I think no one ever really thinks about and often is going to completely miss, because there’s just not a lot of unclaimed property funds out there in your name. Surprise, surprise, there were some for me. It helped me make some money on my first day, and that was pretty cool. I wouldn’t have done that if I hadn’t been prompted by this thing to do it. And there’s a check on its way to me in the mail. Fantastic. [But] that ends pretty quickly, right? That was a one-time shot, but it’s not a thing that’s repeatable. That’s more like a party trick-type thing. Kirsten: I mean, you just killed your own argument. I don’t see how that wouldn’t become wildly popular. Sean: The more sustainable version of that, and the thing that Meta’s talked up a lot over the last couple of days, is taking that idea and applying it to your real, true everyday financials, like giving it your information for your credit card, your Gmail account, all this other stuff, do things that we’ve seen other companies do, like Rocket Money or whatever, where it’ll go cancel subscriptions that you’re not using or identify double charges, things that frankly the credit card company should be doing already. And at that point you just run into that trust wall with Meta. I think one of the reasons that I was willing to explore this and was curious to stick with it a little bit — even through to today — is that somewhat shockingly, when I downloaded it, I just assumed that it would like really instantly prompt me and like plug me right into Threads, Instagram, Facebook, which I don’t really use ever, and pull up that context immediately. But it didn’t. And it was working with me like I was a stranger at first, which made me more willing to use it, because I didn’t feel like Meta had everything on me already. But you can see, as you start to use it, it really tries to grab you and pull those things into the system, so that it can learn all this stuff about you. I don’t know that I will ever trust Meta the same way. I think it’s an interesting timing for me, having just upgraded my iPhone and getting onto the new iOS with the new Siri that actually works and can do some controls on your phone in a way that is surprising and helpful, that it’s never been able to do. [I’ve been] thinking about how much I’ve been using that over the last week and how much more how much more willing I would be to have the Siri version of Muse take that information, because I just trust Apple more with that really sensitive information and not only trust it with the information from a cybersecurity perspective, but from the fact that its business is not to sell me a bunch of crappy ads. Meta’s business is to sell you ads. And yes, they’ll make the argument that the more they know about you, the more accurate and interesting the ads will be — wake me up when we get to that fever dream. And beyond the one-time money lever that I got, which was great, I don’t feel like I’ve found anything else that’s really all that useful — other than the fact that it is, to Anthony’s point, really tailored at keeping it sort of consumer-y in your interactions with it, with which I do think helps it and is why people are talking about it so much. Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and now, more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility! Autonomous vehicle technology is not yet ubiquitous or mainstream. Readers here might shout, “It’s everywhere!” but I can tell you that it is not — although I understand why folks in the San Francisco Bay Area might disagree. The tech is, however, being commercialized and that transition from testing to commercial product has me thinking about scale. A few strategies are emerging. Scale through partnerships. A couple of announcements this week highlighted this strategy. Wayve locked in a commercial partnership with Mercedes-Benz to integrate the startup’s automated driving tech into at least one model set to be deployed within the next two years. This is a Level 2 type product, meaning it handles certain driving maneuvers but still requires the human driver to remain engaged. While this is not a Level 4, or fully driverless product, it gives Wayve reach and follows similar deals with Nissan and Stellantis. Those partnerships have also opened doors for its fully driverless product. Earlier this year, Wayve announced a partnership with Nissan and Uber to launch a robotaxi service in Tokyo. Widespread and concentrated, all at once. As I wrote this week, Waymo’s commercial robotaxi ramp-up looks expansive, both in geographic reach and ridership. And by almost every measure, it is — until you pay attention to where the bulk of those robotaxis are actually showing up. I looked at vehicle registration data and found that, at least for now, Waymo is concentrating its efforts in just two states. About 80% of Waymo’s roughly 4,000 robotaxis are in California and Texas, and Texas is where the action is now: Waymo’s fleet there has grown by more than 49% in the past three weeks. Waymo is also scaling by seeking out new kinds of users: teenagers. I might put Aurora, a company developing and commercializing self-driving trucks, somewhere between these two categories. Aurora is clearly focused on Texas, but it has cast a wide net when it comes to partners. CEO Chris Urmson is also clearly bullish on how the company will scale over the next four years, noting this week that Aurora has “emerged from the building stage.” The company said it’s targeting more than 30,000 driverless trucks in operation by 2030. The company plans to have more than 200 driverless trucks by the end of the year. Our little bird items are typically just that: small yet notable nuggets of insider information from across the transportation industry. But every now and then, a tip turns into something much bigger. That’s what happened a few weeks ago, when Zoox workers reached out to senior reporter Sean O’Kane about a problem with the company’s test fleet in Atlanta. Workers were getting sick, and they suspected it was from its test vehicles, Toyota Highlander SUVs equipped with Zoox’s self-driving system. The TL;DR: Zoox grounded its autonomous vehicle test fleet in Atlanta after safety drivers were potentially exposed to carbon monoxide, carbon dioxide, or hydrogen sulfide gas inside its vehicles last month. Zoox says it only ever found evidence of CO2 in the vehicles.The repeated incidents led one worker to file a complaint with the Occupational Safety and Health Administration, which opened an inquiry and told Zoox to investigate the exposures. Sennheiser Momentum 5 review: Great sound, incredible battery life, and few compromises
2026-09-27

Sennheiser Momentum 5 review: Great sound, incredible battery life, and few compromises

TechCrunch | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. There are a lot of $300-$400 headphones on the market right now, and most of them blur together. I spent the last few weeks with the Sennheiser Momentum 5 to determine if this pair actually stands out, testing everything from sound quality and noise cancellation to comfort and battery life. At $399.99, the Momentum 5 sit right in the middle of the premium headphone market. That puts them up against the pricier Sony WH-1000XM6 and Apple AirPods Max 2, while offering a more affordable option for anyone looking for high-end headphones. Starting with sound, vocals sound natural and the bass is strong without taking over. The headphones allow you to start noticing little things in music, like a background harmony or a guitar string that cheaper headphones often tend to miss. If I had to describe the sound in one word, it’d be balanced. The headphones support Dolby Atmos, which essentially means that music or movies sound like they’re coming from all around you instead of just left and right. With this generation, Sennheiser doubled the microphone count to eight to improve the active noise cancellation (ANC). Voices and nearby chatter, which are usually the kind of sounds that can cut through weaker ANC, are noticeably quieter. Sennheiser claims this pair is up to three times more effective than the previous generation at blocking this type of noise. Of course, it’s not quite at the level of blocking out everything like a plane’s engine, but it does the job effectively without making your ears feel weirdly pressurized as can be the case with some ANC headphones. As for comfort, the headphones aren’t as premium-feeling as Apple or Sony headphones that I’ve tried in the past, but given that the Momentum 5 cost less than its competitors, it’s a trade-off that’s hard to complain about. The battery life is genuinely impressive. It’s rated for up to 57 hours, and that figure held up in my testing. For context, that’s roughly twice the battery life of the Sony WH-1000XM6 and nearly three times that of the AirPods Max 2. And when the battery does run out, a 10-minute charge gets you up to seven hours of playback, while a full charge takes about two hours. A notable difference that the Momentum 5 offers that its competitors don’t is a user-replaceable cell, which means you can swap out the battery once it degrades instead of having to buy a whole new pair. What I wasn’t expecting, but found surprisingly useful, was how thin the carrying case is. I’ve never really been able to fit a pair of headphones into my medium-sized purses, but the Momentum 5 fit perfectly. It’s also a big plus when traveling, since the case doesn’t take up much space in your luggage. With the headphones’ companion app, Smart Control Plus, you can adjust a bunch of different settings. There’s an 8-band EQ that lets you tune the sound yourself or choose from one of Sennheiser’s presets. The app also lets you adjust the level of ANC rather than just switching it on or off, and there’s an anti-wind mode you can access there too. The “Sound Personalization” feature walks you through a listening test and creates a sound profile tailored to your hearing preferences. The app gives you a decent amount of control over the Momentum 5, and I found it pretty easy to navigate as most of the settings are quite straightforward. Overall, the Sennheiser Momentum 5 is a solid option at this price. They offer great sound, the battery life is excellent, and the downsides are pretty minor. They’re heavier than the Sony WH-1000XM6, and aren’t as comfortable as some Sony or Apple options, but at $399.99, they’re cheaper than both while still delivering where it matters. PNOE’s new face mask wants to make lab-grade breath testing a self-serve affair
2026-09-27

PNOE’s new face mask wants to make lab-grade breath testing a self-serve affair

TechCrunch | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. At first glance, the newest device from PNOĒ looks like something a comic-book villain might wear. The mask, which covers the nose and mouth and straps around the back of the head, bears more than a passing resemblance to the one worn by Bane, Batman’s hulking nemesis. But its purpose is far more benign; it measures how much oxygen you consume and how much carbon dioxide you exhale, then turns that data into advice about how to eat, train, and, the company hopes, live longer. PNOĒ, which is based in Malden, Massachusetts, and has operations in Athens, Greece, is preparing to launch the PNOĒ 2.0 on October 1. The big change from its current device is that users can administer the test themselves. According to co-founder and CEO Apostolos Atsalakis, someone can walk into a gym, “just wear the mask, push the button, sit down,” and breathe for eight minutes. “That’s it. It’s that easy,” he said recently, talking with this editor over a Zoom call from the company’s Athens location. That matters because PNOĒ’s current device requires a trained operator, which limits where it can be used. A self-serve version could open the door to fitness centers without dedicated staff and potentially even pharmacies, Atsalakis said. The science behind PNOĒ isn’t new. Metabolic testing, which analyzes the gases in a person’s breath to gauge how their body produces energy, has been around for more than a century. For decades, it has been the gold standard for measuring VO₂ max, the maximum amount of oxygen the body can use during exercise and a widely used measure of cardiorespiratory fitness. But the tests have traditionally required bulky, expensive equipment found mainly in sports labs and hospitals, which is why they’ve largely been the province of elite athletes and executive wellness programs. What 10-year-old PNOĒ promises is the same accuracy in a portable package, paired with software that translates the results into recommendations. “We made it accessible to everyone,” Atsalakis said. The company says its test captures 23 biomarkers, including (beyond measuring VO₂ max) one’s resting metabolic rate (how many calories the body burns at rest), and metabolic flexibility (how well the body switches between burning fat and carbohydrates). Atsalakis argues that these metrics answer questions that blood tests can’t, such as how many calories a person needs or how they should train. The timing is good for PNOĒ. VO₂ max has become a buzzword among longevity enthusiasts, thanks in part to research linking higher cardiorespiratory fitness to lower mortality. Atsalakis calls VO₂ max the strongest predictor of human longevity, and he sees his company’s data as a kind of scorecard for the booming wellness industry. The new device is smaller and more compact than its predecessor, with fewer parts, which Atsalakis said makes it more reliable. It was designed with Milan-based Design Group Italia over what Atsalakis described as “a lot, a lot, a lot of iterations,” since a self-administered metabolic testing device hadn’t been done before. It also addresses a question that post-pandemic users are likely to ask: who wore it last? The answer: it doesn’t matter, as the electronics detach from the silicone mask and straps, so multiple people can share the costly hardware while each user keeps their own mask. For all its clinical ambitions, PNOĒ is careful about what it claims. The device isn’t cleared by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration, and Atsalakis said that “we do not provide medical recommendations.” Instead, PNOĒ considers itself a wellness device. “It’s like a body composition device, like a scale,” he said. “A doctor cannot prescribe medication based on our results.” That could change down the road. Researchers have long explored whether compounds in human breath can signal diseases such as cancer, and Atsalakis believes the company’s growing trove of data could eventually help it flag health issues. But he acknowledged that full diagnoses are “definitely a couple of years away,” with regulatory hurdles likely stretching that timeline further. “We’re not there yet,” he said. PNOĒ traces its roots to Atsalakis’s PhD work in sensing technologies at the University of Cambridge, when wearables were taking off and he became fascinated by what the breath could reveal about the body. He co-founded the company with Panos Papadiamantis, a childhood friend who is now the company’s chief product officer. The startup went through Y Combinator’s Winter 2019 batch, back when “longevity” was not yet the industry it is today. It has since raised about $22 million, including a recently closed $11 million round, from investors including 50 Years and Google Maps co-founder Lars Rasmussen, who is himself now based in Athens. PNOĒ sells only to businesses, which then offer the test to their customers. Its clients include Equinox, where it’s available at almost all clubs, said Atsalakis, as well as Four Seasons hotels, Red Bull, the NBA, the Mount Sinai Health System, and the med spa chain Restore Hyper Wellness. About 85% of its business comes from the U.S., which Atsalakis described as “by far the most advanced market globally” for longevity. The company is now expanding in Europe and, through partners, in Latin America and Asia. Businesses pay a subscription ranging from $400 a month to more than $1,000, which covers the hardware, software, training, and marketing materials, a package Atsalakis calls a “business in a box.” PNOĒ also links its results to the services a business sells, so a gym or spa can recommend specific offerings based on a customer’s test. Many clients use the test during onboarding, Atsalakis said, positioning it somewhere between a full clinical workup and the estimates people get from their smartwatches. That middle ground is increasingly crowded. Apple, Garmin, and Whoop all estimate VO₂ max from heart-rate data, while consumer devices like Lumen analyze breath to gauge fat and carb burning. At the high end, traditional metabolic carts remain the standard in labs and hospitals. PNOĒ, which employs 110 people, says it recently turned profitable, while growing more than 100% a year. Atsalakis said the company plans to raise a Series B within the next six to 12 months as it tries to put its mask — Bane comparisons and all — in front of more faces. Update your preferences in Account Settings British counterterrorism police are leading an investigation after five men were arrested on suspicion of explosives offenses and preparing a terrorist act near an air base used by U.S. forces. Police received a report at around 12:45 a.m. Sunday about three suspicious vehicles traveling toward RAF Fairford in Gloucestershire, southwestern England. Armed officers responded and arrested the men in nearby Whelford. Fairford is used as a forward operating location for the U.S. Air Force Global Strike Command. The base has hosted U.S. B-52 and B-1 bombers during the Iran war, according to ABC News. American forces have used Fairford to carry out strikes against Iranian missile sites, the Associated Press reported. Read More: Why More Republicans Are Breaking With Trump on Iran In a press conference Sunday, Assistant Chief Constable Richard Ocone said that about 85 households in the area were advised to evacuate early Sunday morning as a precaution. "A 400m cordon has been put in place around the vehicles, while the Army's Explosive Ordnance Disposal Unit examine them,” Ocone said. The base is home to both the 501st Combat Support Wing Headquarters and the 420th Air Base Squadron, and serves as the “preferred bomber forward operating location in Europe,” according to the U.S. Air Force.
